I just do it so all that food won't sit in me and turn into weight, you know?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food happen?\n\nPatient: Well, since my apartment move settled into a routine, I'd say it happens most weeks. I usually have at least one of those late-night episodes where I just eat way past what I meant to. And then I try to make up for it... either by throwing up, like I mentioned, or sometimes I'll just skip breakfast the next day, or force myself to go on a really long run.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, quite a bit, actually. Even when people at the venue where I work tell me I look too lean, I still get... well, I get panicky if my pants feel even a little tighter, or if the scale is up just a single pound. I immediately start worrying that the guests and my coworkers will notice right away and think I've let myself go. So, yeah, it's definitely something I stress about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Once I start on those nights, it's almost like... like I'm watching myself from the outside. I'll be sitting there telling myself to put the food away and just stop, but my hands just keep opening containers. I just keep eating until everything is gone. So, yeah, I definitely don't feel in control when it happens.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I'd say... sometimes. Usually, it's after I get home from work and end up eating way too much takeout or a bunch of snacks. I'll just go straight to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I just do it so the food won't sit in me and turn into weight.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since my apartment move settled into a routine, I'd say most weeks I have at least one of those late-night episodes where I eat way past what I meant to. After that happens, I try to make up for it... so I'll throw up, or maybe I'll skip breakfast the next morning, or force myself to go on a really long run. So, to answer your question, it's usually at least once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you use laxatives or diuretics to try to make up for the amount you ate during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't use anything like that. I just... well, like I said, I either throw up, skip a meal, or run it off. But no laxatives or water pills or anything.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without trying to make up for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I don't really... I don't think that ever happens. If I end up eating a huge amount of food like that, I always feel like I have to do something about it. I can't just let it sit there. So, no, I don't really have times where I eat like that and just leave it be. I always try to make up for it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without throwing up afterwards?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... like I mentioned a minute ago, I don't always throw up. Sometimes after a bad night of eating, I'll just make myself run extra miles on the trail the next morning instead. Or I'll skip breakfast before my shift starts. I'll do that even when I'm really tired, just because it feels like I have to undo what I ate. So, if I don't throw up, I'm definitely doing one of those other things to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short time without doing anything to make up for it, such as throwing up, skipping meals, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: Like I think I just mentioned... that never happens. I don't ever eat a huge amount of food like that and just do nothing. I always have to do at least one of those things to fix it—either the throwing up, the running, or skipping a meal. I just... I can't let it sit there. So, yeah, never.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without doing anything to make up for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I think we're going in circles here. Like I said before, I never do that. If I eat a ton of food like that, I always do something to get rid of it or burn it off. There's never a time where I just eat and do nothing afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time and then do nothing at all to try to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Look, doc... I really don't know how else to answer that. No. Like I just said, the answer is never. I always do something to make up for it. I never just eat like that and do nothing. Is there... I mean, is there a reason you keep asking me the exact same thing?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
